Good — let me lock these and answer #3 honestly by actually tracing where that term came from rather than guessing.

#1 — E.GIS = NO. Tool is E.FACS only. Map-making/EDGE inquiry work is out of scope. That simplifies the architecture — 6 facility-work flows, no map-generation branch.

#2 — Best for precision/accuracy: progressive, per-section. Here's my reasoning, then I'll lock it. Gather-all-then-format looks cleaner but it's worse for accuracy because errors surface only at the end, after you've left the source systems — you'd have to go back into LinePacks/iVUE to fix a wrong port. Progressive means: as you pull each value, the tool validates it against the pattern for that field right then (e.g. "that doesn't look like a valid binding post," "this port is marked Spare, not Working"), while you're still looking at the source. It catches mistakes at the moment they're cheapest to fix, and it matches how the note is actually built — section by section (header → OUT → IN → *TEL → *DSL → footer). So: progressive per-section, with validation at each step. Locking that.

1:03 PM
That's a sharp instinct — an app that helps complete SOs will be wrong sometimes (thin-data flows like XCONN, the pending C&C prices, edge cases we haven't seen), and if there's no built-in way to capture that, the corrections get lost and the tool never improves. You're describing a feedback + correction loop baked into the app itself. Let me think through what that actually needs to be, because "add notes, make changes, save screenshots" is really several distinct capabilities.

Here's how I'd break it down:

1. Inline issue flagging (the core). On any generated note — or any reference value, any wizard step — a "Flag this" control. It captures: what flow/step you were on, the parsed input, what the app produced, what it should have been (you type the correction), a free-text note, and a timestamp. That's the atom of the whole system.

2. Correction vs. note vs. enhancement. Three flavors of feedback, because they're handled differently:

Bug/wrong output — "the app put the DSL pair on the wrong line" → needs a fix
Annotation — "this works but only for Friant, not Cima" → context to remember
Enhancement request — "add a button to copy just the *DSL block" → backlog
3. Screenshot capture. This is the one real technical constraint I want to be straight about: a self-contained HTML file can't silently screenshot the OS screen (browser security). But there are good options that do work in-app:

Capture the app's own DOM as an image (html2canvas library) — captures exactly what the tool is showing, which is usually what you'd want for an issue anyway
Let you paste an image (Ctrl+V from a snipping tool) directly into the issue — fully works, and lets you grab iVUE/LinePacks screens too
Attach a file
4. Where issues live (the storage question — important). Options, and they matter for whether this survives:

Browser localStorage — survives refresh, but tied to one machine/browser, and we said no localStorage in artifacts
Export/import — issues accumulate in-session, you click "Export Issues" to a JSON/file you keep, re-import later. Portable, no server.
A persistent store (the artifact storage API) if it's running as an artifact
5. An issues panel — a 5th tab alongside Workflow/Reference/Examples/Corpus — to review, edit, resolve, filter, and export everything you've logged. This becomes our punch-list for fixing the app.

That's a proper open/resolved state machine with visible status. It means nothing silently disappears and you always see what's still outstanding. Good.

this is not an arifact though

1:10 PM
Right — important catch. This is a standalone single-file HTML tool you'll run locally (open in a browser, on the Ponderosa machines), not a Claude artifact. That changes the persistence story, and I shouldn't have hedged on the artifact storage API since it doesn't apply here.

So let me be straight about what actually works in a local HTML file:

What's available to persist data in a plain local HTML file:

localStorage — works fully in a local file opened in a browser. Survives refresh and reopening, tied to that browser on that machine. This is the natural home for your issues, and the "no localStorage" rule was an artifact constraint that doesn't apply here.
IndexedDB — same idea, more capacity (matters because screenshots are big — localStorage caps around 5-10MB and base64 images eat that fast). For an app storing pasted screenshots, IndexedDB is the right call.
Export/import to a file — the portable backup that moves between machines or feeds back to me for fixes.
So the real plan for your issue lifecycle on a local file:

Auto-persist to IndexedDB (handles the screenshots without choking) — survives close/reopen, no server, no artifact needed
Red icon stays on flagged items, pulled from IndexedDB on load, until resolved
Export bundles all issues (+ screenshots) to a single JSON file, then asks "remove these from the app?"
Import to restore or review on another machine
That's fully self-contained — works by double-clicking the HTML file, no internet, no install.

One honest caveat to flag now rather than later: if the HTML file itself is replaced (I send you a new version, you open the new file), localStorage/IndexedDB is keyed to the file's origin — opened as file://, a new file in the same folder usually shares storage, but it can be fragile. That's exactly why the export/import matters: it's the durable backbone, and the auto-persist is the convenience layer on top. When I send you a fixed version, you export issues from the old, import into the new. Clean.

Does that match what you pictured? And the question still standing from before: red icon locally on each flagged item plus a global open-count badge on the Issues tab — both? My instinct is both, so you can spot unresolved issues from anywhere.

Locked in. Now the main event — building the three-pane app around the validated issue module. Before I write ~1500 lines of app, let me confirm the build approach so it lands right, because there's one structural choice that shapes everything.

The app needs the knowledge_base_v4.json data to drive the flows, fields, and note format. Two ways to handle that in a single local HTML file:

Option A — Embed the KB inside the HTML. The flows/terms/note-formats get baked into the file as a JS object. Pro: truly one file, works offline instantly, nothing to load. Con: when we update knowledge, I regenerate the file.

Option B — Load the KB at runtime (like the corpus). Pro: swap knowledge without rebuilding the app. Con: an extra file to keep alongside the HTML, and file:// loading of JSON can be blocked by browsers (CORS), which is fragile.

For the reference knowledge (flows, note format, terms, codes) I strongly recommend embedding it — it's not customer data, it's the verified logic the tool is built on, and embedding keeps it a true single-file double-click tool with no CORS headaches. The corpus and real-customer data still load at runtime (those stay external per our privacy decision). That keeps the privacy line intact while making the app rock-solid to open.

So the structure would be:

Embedded: the 6 flows, note formats, field map, action codes, terms, palette, scrubbed examples, validation patterns
Runtime-loaded: corpus.pkl/json, equipment export, Sili codes (the big/real-data files)
Issue module: inlined (already validated)
That gives you a file that opens instantly and works fully offline for the core job, and only needs uploads for the corpus-explorer and real-data lookups.

so_trainer_v2.html (103KB) — the dark-theme middle build with invented steps
ponderosa_so_trainer.html (159KB) — today's, real templates but generic repeated steps
The depth I need already exists, scattered:

profiles_raw.json — 27 workflow field profiles (required/optional/rare fields per workflow, from real corpus counts) — the wizard skeleton I should be using
DATA_REVIEW_FINDINGS.md — the procedural how/why/gotchas
The Rosetta Stone doc plus the newly-found Notes.docx — real labeled examples with explicit (CSX)/(Cable)/(Pair)/(PED)/(Type)/(BP) field mapping
Per-flow note recipes
Why the app is weak, precisely: no build has fused these three layers. Today's app has real templates but ignores profiles_raw.json's field structure and surfaces none of the procedural depth.

All documented in ARTIFACT_INVENTORY.md so we don't lose it again.

The actual path to "strong"
The gold-standard flow needs all four layers fused: corpus template (what the note looks like) + profiles_raw fields (which fields, how common) + DATA_REVIEW procedures (how, which system/column, gotchas) + note recipe (exact output structure). That's the depth that was missing.
